Loop_only ≈ full_trace_label_only (85.63% vs 86.22%) confirms VOTA's loop compression precisely extracts the useful behavioral signal from full traces.\\n\\n**Supporting evidence:**\\n- `vota_exception_only_seed42_v2`: 99.83%, ECE=0.0027 (Tier 1 exception)\\n- `vota_ae_only_seed42`: 99.92%, ECE=0.0004 (Tier 1 assertion-point, 与 exception 统计等价 p=0.80)\\n- `vota_loop_only_seed42`: 85.63%, ECE=0.0831 (Tier 2 behavioral)\\n- `full_trace_label_only_seed42`: 86.22%, ECE=0.1107 (≈ loop_only, confirming extraction equivalence)\\n- `deepseek_exception_only_seed42`: 99.83% epoch1 / 99.33% epoch3 (Tier 1 cross-model validation)\\n- `deepseek_vota_seed42`: 84.45% (Tier 1+2 cross-model: Tier 2 introduces model-dependent degradation)\\n\\n**Calibration**: 完整 calibration 数据确认 Tier 1 信号不仅 accuracy 近完美，置信度校准也近完美：\\n\\n| Condition | Accuracy | ECE | Brier | 解读 |\\n|-----------|----------|-----|-------|------|\\n| ae_only | 99.92% | 0.0004 | 0.0008 | 近乎完美校准 |\\n| exception_only | 99.83% | 0.0027 | 0.0013 | 近乎完美校准 |\\n| VOTA | 99.41% | 0.0054 | 0.005 | 优秀，但 Tier 2 引入轻微 noise |\\n| loop_only | 85.63% | 0.0831 | — | Tier 2 单独校准中等 |\\n| label_only | 86.22% | 0.1107 | — | gradient dilution fix 仍 miscalibrated |\\n| full_trace | 81.76% | 0.0958 | — | 中度 miscalibrated |\\n| no_trace | 75.13% | 0.1715 | 0.1972 | 严重 miscalibrated |\\n\\nno_trace ECE 是 exception_only 的 **~63x**（0.1715/0.0027），Brier 是 **~150x**（0.1972/0.0013）。Trace signal（尤其 Tier 1）不仅提升 accuracy，更从根本上改善模型 calibration。\\n\\n**Design principle**: verdict-optimized traces should extract highest-specificity diagnostic signals;   "project": "trace-verdict-study"
```json
}
```

```text
{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Experiments}\\n\\nWe evaluate our trace preprocessing pipeline across two research questions:\\n(1)~Do Tier~1 exception signals generalize across models of different capability?\\n(2)~What is the relative contribution of each trace component to verdict prediction accuracy?\\nAll experiments use our trace--verdict dataset (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:dataset}) with $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples; accuracy is reported with Wilson 95\\\\% confidence intervals unless stated otherwise.\\nPairwise comparisons use paired bootstrap resampling ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42); all cross-condition differences reported below are significant at $p{<}0.0001$ unless noted otherwise.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Cross-Model Comparison}\\n\\\\label{sec:cross_model}\\n\\nTo test whether exception-based trace compression transfers across model families, we compare Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct under four input conditions: \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (Tier~1 exception events), VOTA (exception + assertion-point + loop compression), \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} (complete execution trace), and \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} (source code only).\\n\\n\\\\begin{table}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\small\\n\\\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\\n\\\\toprule\\n\\\\textbf{Condition} & \\\\textbf{Qwen-2.5-7B} & \\\\textbf{DeepSeek-6.7B} & \\\\textbf{Gap} \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\midrule\\nexception\\\\_only & \\\\textbf{99.83}\\\\% & 99.33\\\\%$^\\\\dag$ & 0.50pp \\\\\\\\\\nVOTA & 99.41\\\\% & 84.45\\\\% & 14.96pp \\\\\\\\\\nVOTA\\\\_no\\\\_ae & 99.41\\\\%$^\\\\S$ & --- & --- \\\\\\\\\\nfull\\\\_trace & 81.76\\\\% & 79.16\\\\% & 2.60pp \\\\\\\\\\nno\\\\_trace & 75.13\\\\% & 73.36\\\\% & 1.77pp \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\midrule\\nMajority baseline & 71.51\\\\% & 71.51\\\\% & --- \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\bottomrule\\n\\\\end{tabular}\\n\\\\caption{Cross-model accuracy ($N{=}1{,}190$). Qwen CIs: exception\\\\_only [99.58, 100.00], VOTA [98.82, 99.83], full\\\\_trace [79.70, 83.90], no\\\\_trace [72.77, 77.48]; DeepSeek CIs: full\\\\_trace [76.80, 81.30], VOTA [82.60, 86.40], no\\\\_trace [70.80, 75.70]. $^\\\\dag$DeepSeek \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} 3-seed results: 99.33\\\\% (seeds 42, 456; CI [98.82, 99.75]) and 91.09\\\\% (seed 123; CI [89.40, 92.60]); seed 123 is a LoRA initialization outlier (fail recall = 70.5\\\\% vs.\\\\ 100\\\\%). $^\\\\S$VOTA\\\\_no\\\\_ae evaluated on Qwen only; CI [98.90, 99.80].}\\n\\\\label{tab:cross_model}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\nTable~\\\\ref{tab:cross_model} reveals a striking asymmetry.\\nUnder \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}, Qwen achieves 99.83\\\\% $\\\\pm$ 0.00 across three seeds, while DeepSeek shows a bimodal pattern: seeds 42 and 456 both achieve 99.33\\\\% (gap = 0.50pp), while seed 123 yields 91.09\\\\% (gap = 8.74pp), attributable to suboptimal LoRA initialization (fail recall = 70.5\\\\% vs.\\\\ 100\\\\% for other seeds).\\nEven the outlier seed outperforms VOTA (84.45\\\\%) by 6.64pp, confirming exception events as the most effective component across models.\\nUnder VOTA, the gap widens to 14.96pp: DeepSeek drops to 84.45\\\\% while Qwen remains at 99.41\\\\%.\\nPost-hoc tokenizer analysis reveals a confound: measured on the training set using each model's tokenizer, the DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}626 tokens per VOTA sample versus 1{,}908 for Qwen, causing 91.7\\\\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window.\\nThe VOTA training framework preserves the verdict label but truncates the trace content (input), so the model trains with incomplete diagnostic information for the vast majority of the dataset.\\nThe 14.96pp gap thus reflects both model capability differences and training-time trace truncation; the two factors cannot be disentangled in the current design.\\nHowever, a post-hoc group analysis provides directional evidence: partitioning DeepSeek VOTA test samples by whether their training-set counterparts exceeded the context window, intact samples ($N{=}99$) achieve 100\\\\% accuracy while truncated samples ($N{=}1{,}091$) achieve 83.04\\\\% (95\\\\% CI [80.75\\\\%, 85.24\\\\%]; Fisher exact $p{<}0.001$), with all 185 errors concentrated in the fail class of the truncated group (fail recall $=$ 31.23\\\\%).\\nIn contrast, the \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} condition is unaffected by truncation because exception-based inputs are short enough to fit within the context window for both token\\n\\n... [截断: 输出过长，省略 8322 字符] ...\\n\\nn{Calibration metrics on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). ECE = expected calibration error; Brier = Brier score. Lower is better for both.}\\n\\\\label{tab:calibration}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\nTable~\\\\ref{tab:calibration} shows that calibration quality mirrors the Tier~1/2 hierarchy.\\nTier~1 conditions (\\\\texttt{ae\\\\_only}: ECE=0.0004; \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}: ECE=0.003) produce near-perfectly calibrated predictions, while \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} (ECE=0.096) and \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} (ECE=0.171) exhibit substantial miscalibration.\\nVOTA (ECE=0.005) is well-calibrated but slightly worse than either Tier~1 component alone, reinforcing the pattern that combining trace signals dilutes rather than improves the prediction signal.\\n\\nThe Brier scores tell a consistent story: \\\\texttt{ae\\\\_only} (0.0008) and \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (0.0013) achieve near-zero scores, while \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} (0.197) indicates substantial miscalibration.\\n\\nFigure~\\\\ref{fig:cross_model_gap} visualizes the cross-model gap pattern, showing that exception-based conditions maintain tight cross-model agreement while VOTA and lower-tier conditions diverge.\\n\\n\\\\begin{figure}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\includegraphics[width=\\\\columnwidth]{fig2_calibration}\\n\\\\caption{Reliability diagrams for four trace conditions on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). The Tier~1 condition (\\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}) clusters near the diagonal (well-calibrated), while \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} and \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} exhibit systematic overconfidence.}\\n\\\\label{fig:calibration}\\n\\\\end{figure}\\n\\n\\\\begin{figure}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\includegraphics[width=\\\\columnwidth]{fig3_cross_model_gap}\\n\\\\caption{Cross-model accuracy gap (Qwen $-$ DeepSeek) across trace conditions. Exception-based signals transfer across models (0.50pp gap), while VOTA is model-dependent (14.96pp gap).}\\n\\\\label{fig:cross_model_gap}\\n\\\\end{figure}\\n\\\\subsection{Cross-Dataset Validation: DebugBench}\\n\\\\label{sec:debugbench}\\n\\nTo test whether the component hierarchy generalizes beyond our primary dataset, we evaluate on DebugBench \\\\citep{tian2024debugbench}, a collection of 426 buggy Python code snippets spanning logic, reference, and syntax bugs.\\nWe train separate Qwen-2.5-7B models for each condition using DebugBench's training split and evaluate on its test split.\\n\\nThe results confirm the hierarchy observed on HumanEval+MBPP.\\n\\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} achieves 100\\\\% accuracy (426/426; CI [99.14, 100.00]), VOTA achieves 99.77\\\\% (425/426; CI [99.30, 100.00]), and \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} achieves 96.71\\\\% (CI [94.80, 98.40]).\\nThe component ranking is preserved: exception events $\\\\geq$ VOTA $>$ \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace}.\\nThe single VOTA error and 14 \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} errors indicate that DebugBench's diverse bug types are equally well-captured by exception signals, consistent with the primary dataset findings.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Regex Baseline Comparison}\\n\\\\label{sec:regex_baseline}\\n\\nA natural question is whether the \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} signal can be captured by simple pattern matching without fine-tuning.\\nWe evaluate five regex baselines that detect error patterns in execution traces: B1 (``ERROR'' string presence, 93.45\\\\%), B2 (``Traceback'' detection, 71.51\\\\%), B3 (any error keyword, 93.45\\\\%), B4 (exception type extraction, 93.61\\\\%), and B5 (format marker ``[FAIL]''/``[PASS]'', 99.33\\\\%).\\n\\nThe strongest \\\\emph{legitimate} regex baseline (B4, matching exception type names) achieves 93.61\\\\%, 6.22pp below SFT \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (99.83\\\\%; McNemar $p{=}0.041$).\\nB5 achieves 99.33\\\\% but exploits a format artifact: the ``[FAIL]''/``[PASS]'' markers are inserted by our trace preprocessing pipeline, not by the Python runtime, so its accuracy reflects format leakage rather than genuine trace comprehension.\\nThe SFT advantage over legitimate regex baselines confirms that the fine-tuned model captures exception semantics beyond simple string matching---specifically, the model learns to interpret exception context (type, message, location) jointly, whereas regex baselines treat each pattern independently.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.49}}"}
```

```json
      "result": "{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Experiments}\\n\\nWe evaluate our trace preprocessing pipeline across two research questions:\\n(1)~Do Tier~1 exception signals generalize across models of different capability?\\n(2)~What is the relative contribution of each trace component to verdict prediction accuracy?\\nAll experiments use our trace--verdict dataset (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:dataset}) with $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples; accuracy is reported with Wilson 95\\\\% confidence intervals unless stated otherwise.\\nPairwise comparisons use paired bootstrap resampling ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42); all cross-condition differences reported below are significant at $p{<}0.0001$ unless noted otherwise.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Cross-Model Comparison}\\n\\\\label{sec:cross_model}\\n\\nTo test whether exception-based trace compression transfers across model families, we compare Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct under four input conditions: \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (Tier~1 exception events), VOTA (exception + assertion-point + loop compression), \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} (complete execution trace), and \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} (source code only).\\n\\n\\\\begin{table}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\small\\n\\\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\\n\\\\toprule\\n\\\\textbf{Condition} & \\\\textbf{Qwen-2.5-7B} & \\\\textbf{DeepSeek-6.7B} & \\\\textbf{Gap} \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\midrule\\nexception\\\\_only & \\\\textbf{99.83}\\\\% & 99.33\\\\%$^\\\\dag$ & 0.50pp \\\\\\\\\\nVOTA & 99.41\\\\% & 84.45\\\\% & 14.96pp \\\\\\\\\\nVOTA\\\\_no\\\\_ae & 99.41\\\\%$^\\\\S$ & --- & --- \\\\\\\\\\nfull\\\\_trace & 81.76\\\\% & 79.16\\\\% & 2.60pp \\\\\\\\\\nno\\\\_trace & 75.13\\\\% & 73.36\\\\% & 1.77pp \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\midrule\\nMajority baseline & 71.51\\\\% & 71.51\\\\% & --- \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\bottomrule\\n\\\\end{tabular}\\n\\\\caption{Cross-model accuracy ($N{=}1{,}190$). Qwen CIs: exception\\\\_only [99.58, 100.00], VOTA [98.82, 99.83], full\\\\_trace [79.70, 83.90], no\\\\_trace [72.77, 77.48]; DeepSeek CIs: full\\\\_trace [76.80, 81.30], VOTA [82.60, 86.40], no\\\\_trace [70.80, 75.70]. $^\\\\dag$DeepSeek \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} 3-seed results: 99.33\\\\% (seeds 42, 456; CI [98.82, 99.75]) and 91.09\\\\% (seed 123; CI [89.40, 92.60]); seed 123 is a LoRA initialization outlier (fail recall = 70.5\\\\% vs.\\\\ 100\\\\%). $^\\\\S$VOTA\\\\_no\\\\_ae evaluated on Qwen only; CI [98.90, 99.80].}\\n\\\\label{tab:cross_model}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\nTable~\\\\ref{tab:cross_model} reveals a striking asymmetry.\\nUnder \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}, Qwen achieves 99.83\\\\% $\\\\pm$ 0.00 across three seeds, while DeepSeek shows a bimodal pattern: seeds 42 and 456 both achieve 99.33\\\\% (gap = 0.50pp), while seed 123 yields 91.09\\\\% (gap = 8.74pp), attributable to suboptimal LoRA initialization (fail recall = 70.5\\\\% vs.\\\\ 100\\\\% for other seeds).\\nEven the outlier seed outperforms VOTA (84.45\\\\%) by 6.64pp, confirming exception events as the most effective component across models.\\nUnder VOTA, the gap widens to 14.96pp: DeepSeek drops to 84.45\\\\% while Qwen remains at 99.41\\\\%.\\nPost-hoc tokenizer analysis reveals a confound: measured on the training set using each model's tokenizer, the DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}626 tokens per VOTA sample versus 1{,}908 for Qwen, causing 91.7\\\\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window.\\nThe VOTA training framework preserves the verdict label but truncates the trace content (input), so the model trains with incomplete diagnostic information for the vast majority of the dataset.\\nThe 14.96pp gap thus reflects both model capability differences and training-time trace truncation; the two factors cannot be disentangled in the current design.\\nHowever, a post-hoc group analysis provides directional evidence: partitioning DeepSeek VOTA test samples by whether their training-set counterparts exceeded the context window, intact samples ($N{=}99$) achieve 100\\\\% accuracy while truncated samples ($N{=}1{,}091$) achieve 83.04\\\\% (95\\\\% CI [80.75\\\\%, 85.24\\\\%]; Fisher exact $p{<}0.001$), with all 185 errors concentrated in the fail class of the truncated group (fail recall $=$ 31.23\\\\%).\\nIn contrast, the \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} condition is unaffected by truncation because exception-based inputs are short enough to fit within the context window for both token\\n\\n... [截断: 输出过长，省略 8322 字符] ...\\n\\nn{Calibration metrics on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). ECE = expected calibration error; Brier = Brier score. Lower is better for both.}\\n\\\\label{tab:calibration}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\nTable~\\\\ref{tab:calibration} shows that calibration quality mirrors the Tier~1/2 hierarchy.\\nTier~1 conditions (\\\\texttt{ae\\\\_only}: ECE=0.0004; \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}: ECE=0.003) produce near-perfectly calibrated predictions, while \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} (ECE=0.096) and \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} (ECE=0.171) exhibit substantial miscalibration.\\nVOTA (ECE=0.005) is well-calibrated but slightly worse than either Tier~1 component alone, reinforcing the pattern that combining trace signals dilutes rather than improves the prediction signal.\\n\\nThe Brier scores tell a consistent story: \\\\texttt{ae\\\\_only} (0.0008) and \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (0.0013) achieve near-zero scores, while \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} (0.197) indicates substantial miscalibration.\\n\\nFigure~\\\\ref{fig:cross_model_gap} visualizes the cross-model gap pattern, showing that exception-based conditions maintain tight cross-model agreement while VOTA and lower-tier conditions diverge.\\n\\n\\\\begin{figure}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\includegraphics[width=\\\\columnwidth]{fig2_calibration}\\n\\\\caption{Reliability diagrams for four trace conditions on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). The Tier~1 condition (\\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}) clusters near the diagonal (well-calibrated), while \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} and \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} exhibit systematic overconfidence.}\\n\\\\label{fig:calibration}\\n\\\\end{figure}\\n\\n\\\\begin{figure}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\includegraphics[width=\\\\columnwidth]{fig3_cross_model_gap}\\n\\\\caption{Cross-model accuracy gap (Qwen $-$ DeepSeek) across trace conditions. Exception-based signals transfer across models (0.50pp gap), while VOTA is model-dependent (14.96pp gap).}\\n\\\\label{fig:cross_model_gap}\\n\\\\end{figure}\\n\\\\subsection{Cross-Dataset Validation: DebugBench}\\n\\\\label{sec:debugbench}\\n\\nTo test whether the component hierarchy generalizes beyond our primary dataset, we evaluate on DebugBench \\\\citep{tian2024debugbench}, a collection of 426 buggy Python code snippets spanning logic, reference, and syntax bugs.\\nWe train separate Qwen-2.5-7B models for each condition using DebugBench's training split and evaluate on its test split.\\n\\nThe results confirm the hierarchy observed on HumanEval+MBPP.\\n\\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} achieves 100\\\\% accuracy (426/426; CI [99.14, 100.00]), VOTA achieves 99.77\\\\% (425/426; CI [99.30, 100.00]), and \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} achieves 96.71\\\\% (CI [94.80, 98.40]).\\nThe component ranking is preserved: exception events $\\\\geq$ VOTA $>$ \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace}.\\nThe single VOTA error and 14 \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} errors indicate that DebugBench's diverse bug types are equally well-captured by exception signals, consistent with the primary dataset findings.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Regex Baseline Comparison}\\n\\\\label{sec:regex_baseline}\\n\\nA natural question is whether the \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} signal can be captured by simple pattern matching without fine-tuning.\\nWe evaluate five regex baselines that detect error patterns in execution traces: B1 (``ERROR'' string presence, 93.45\\\\%), B2 (``Traceback'' detection, 71.51\\\\%), B3 (any error keyword, 93.45\\\\%), B4 (exception type extraction, 93.61\\\\%), and B5 (format marker ``[FAIL]''/``[PASS]'', 99.33\\\\%).\\n\\nThe strongest \\\\emph{legitimate} regex baseline (B4, matching exception type names) achieves 93.61\\\\%, 6.22pp below SFT \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (99.83\\\\%; McNemar $p{=}0.041$).\\nB5 achieves 99.33\\\\% but exploits a format artifact: the ``[FAIL]''/``[PASS]'' markers are inserted by our trace preprocessing pipeline, not by the Python runtime, so its accuracy reflects format leakage rather than genuine trace comprehension.\\nThe SFT advantage over legitimate regex baselines confirms that the fine-tuned model captures exception semantics beyond simple string matching---specifically, the model learns to interpret exception context (type, message, location) jointly, whereas regex baselines treat each pattern independently.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.49}}"
```

```json
{
  "text": "## 审查报告 — worker_paper_patch-regex-debugbench（论文数据核实）\n\n**结论**: WARNING（1 个统计归属错误需修正，其余数字准确）\n\n### 发现\n\n**[severity: warning] Regex Baseline 节 McNemar p 值归属错误**\n\n论文写道：\"The strongest legitimate regex baseline (B4, matching exception type names) achieves 93.61%, 6.22pp below SFT exception_only (99.83%; McNemar p=0.041).\"\n\n但 p=0.041 实际是 **B5（format marker, 99.33%） vs SFT（99.83%）** 的 McNemar p 值，不是 B4 vs SFT。证据：\n- `regex_baseline_results.json` 明确记录 `best_overall_regex.mcnemar_p_vs_sft: 0.041`，对应 B5（99.33%）\n- Registry 中字段名为 `B5_mcnemar_p: 0.041`\n- B4（93.61%）vs SFT（99.83%）的 McNemar p 值应远小于 0.001（~74 个不一致对，χ² ≈ 74，p < 1e-17）\n\n**审稿人攻击面**：任何审稿人对 93.61% vs 99.83% 报 p=0.041 都会觉得可疑——6.22pp 差距在 n=1190 上不可能只有 0.041。这会引发数据完整性质疑。\n\n**建议修正**：\n- 方案 A：补算 B4 vs SFT 的 McNemar p 值（应 < 0.001），替换 0.041\n- 方案 B：将 p=0.041 正确归属给 B5 vs SFT 比较，单独报告\n\n---\n\n**[severity: info] 其余数字全部通过核实**\n\n逐项交叉验证 experiments.tex + appendix.tex 与 registry：\n\n| 数据点 | 论文 | Registry | 状态 |\n|--------|------|----------|------|\n| Qwen exception_only | 99.83% | 0.9983 | ✅ |\n| Qwen VOTA | 99.41% | 0.9941 | ✅ |\n| Qwen full_trace | 81.76% | 0.8176 | ✅ |\n| Qwen no_trace | 75.13% | 0.7513 | ✅ |\n| DeepSeek exception_only | 99.33% | 0.9933 | ✅ |\n| DeepSeek VOTA | 84.45% | 0.8445 | ✅ |\n| DeepSeek full_trace | 79.16% | 0.7916 | ✅ |\n| DeepSeek no_trace | 73.36% | 0.7336 | ✅ |\n| DebugBench exc_only | 100% (426/426) | 1.0, n=426 | ✅ |\n| DebugBench VOTA | 99.77% (425/426) | 0.9977, n=426 | ✅ |\n| DebugBench no_trace | 96.71% | 0.9671 | ✅ |\n| Regex B1-B5 所有准确率 | 匹配 | 匹配 | ✅ |\n| Calibration ECE/Brier 所有值 | 匹配（合理四舍五入） | 匹配 | ✅ |\n| 组件消融全部（ae_only/loop_only 等） | 匹配 | 匹配 | ✅ |\n\n**[severity: info] 论文结构评价**：regex baseline 对 B5 format leakage 的讨论诚实透明；DebugBench 作为 cross-dataset validation 定位恰当（no_trace baseline 96.71% 已很高，marginal contribution 3.29pp 适合支撑泛化而非核心 claim）；per-class breakdown 和 training details appendix 信息完整。",
```

```text
{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Introduction}\\n\\nExecution traces---line-by-line records of a program's runtime behavior---have emerged as a promising input modality for code language models.\\nRecent work has explored trace-augmented training for code generation \\\\citep{chen2025tracepile}, execution-aware pretraining \\\\citep{ding2024traced}, trace-based code understanding \\\\citep{wang2025dcsh}, and self-training on trace chain-of-thought \\\\citep{ni2024next}.\\nYet a fundamental question remains open: \\\\emph{which components of an execution trace actually matter?}\\n\\n\\\\citet{wang2025dcsh} found that no single trace representation consistently outperforms others on generation-oriented tasks, concluding that the utility of execution traces is task- and format-dependent.\\nHowever, their study focused on code generation and repair---tasks that require the model to simulate or reconstruct execution semantics.\\n\\\\emph{Verdict prediction}---classifying whether a test passes or fails---is a fundamentally different task type: it is a fault detection problem, not a generation problem.\\nWhether the same trace components matter for fault detection as for generation is unknown.\\n\\nWe present the first systematic study of what trace components drive verdict prediction performance.\\nUsing 591 problems from HumanEval \\\\citep{chen2021evaluating} and MBPP-sanitized \\\\citep{austin2021program} with model-generated solutions and execution traces, we decompose a multi-component trace representation (VOTA: exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression) into individual components and evaluate each in isolation across two models: Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct.\\n\\nOur results reveal a striking finding: \\\\textbf{exception events alone achieve near-perfect verdict prediction on Qwen (99.83\\\\% $\\\\pm$ 0.00, 3 seeds) and are the most effective component on DeepSeek, with 2/3 seeds matching Qwen-level accuracy (99.33\\\\%); the remaining seed (91.09\\\\%) reflects LoRA initialization sensitivity rather than a signal limitation.}\\nEven the DeepSeek outlier seed (91.09\\\\%) outperforms VOTA (84.45\\\\%) and no-trace (73.36\\\\%) by wide margins, confirming exception events as the dominant trace component across models.\\nIn contrast, the full VOTA representation---which combines exception events with assertion-point and loop information---degrades by 14.96pp on DeepSeek.\\nPost-hoc analysis reveals the mechanism: the DeepSeek tokenizer produces longer sequences than Qwen for identical inputs, causing 91.7\\\\% of VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window; the training framework preserves the verdict label but truncates the trace content, so the model trains on incomplete diagnostic information.\\n\\nThese results reframe verdict prediction as fault detection: the minimal sufficient signal is the exception event, a compact structured diagnostic moment that both models can interpret without deep trace reasoning.\\nWe further decompose the gap between full trace and VOTA into two mechanisms---\\\\emph{gradient dilution} (26\\\\% of the gap) and \\\\emph{information overload} (74\\\\%)---showing that the primary bottleneck is not training signal quality but the presence of irrelevant trace content.\\n\\nWe note that the finding that exception events dominate verdict prediction is not itself surprising---exceptions are, by definition, fault indicators.\\nThe novelty lies in three less obvious results: (1)~adding more trace information actively \\\\emph{hurts} performance (VOTA $<$ \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}), (2)~we quantify \\\\emph{why} through a 26/74 gradient-dilution/information-overload decomposition, and (3)~the VOTA cross-model gap is confounded by tokenizer-driven trace truncation: the DeepSeek tokenizer causes the majority of VOTA training samples to exceed the context window (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:cross_model}), truncating diagnostic trace content while preserving the verdict label.\\n\\nOur contributions are:\\n\\\\begin{enumerate}\\n\\\\item \\\\textbf{Exception signal transfer}: We show that exception events alone achieve 99.83\\\\% $\\\\pm$ 0.00 on Qwen (3 seeds) and 99.33\\\\% on 2/3 DeepSeek seeds, with one outlier at 91.09\\\\% from LoRA initialization sensitivity; even the outlier exceeds VOTA (84.45\\\\%) by 6.64pp (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:cross_model}).\\n\\\\item \\\\textbf{Information density decomposition}: We decompose the full-trace-to-VOTA gap into gradient dilution (26\\\\%) and information overload (74\\\\%), identifying the primary bottleneck for trace-based prediction (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:density}).\\n\\\\item \\\\textbf{Two-tier diagnostic hierarchy}: Component ablation reveals that Tier~1 signals (exception events, assertion points) individually achieve {"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Method}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Task Definition}\\n\\nWe study \\\\emph{verdict prediction}: given a Python function, a test input, and an optional execution trace, predict whether the test passes or fails.\\nThe model produces a single token---\\\\texttt{pass} or \\\\texttt{fail}---making this a binary classification task.\\nUnlike code generation \\\\citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \\\\citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates the model's ability to detect faults from its ability to simulate execution, providing a cleaner lens on what trace information actually contributes.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Dataset}\\n\\\\label{sec:dataset}\\n\\nWe sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\\\\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \\\\citep{chen2021evaluating} (164 problems) and MBPP-sanitized \\\\citep{austin2021program} (427 problems), executed each solution against canonical test cases with \\\\texttt{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level execution traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 trace--verdict pairs (67\\\\% pass, 33\\\\% fail).\\nThe dataset is split 80/20 by problem ID into 4,720 training and 1,190 test samples; the test set majority-class baseline is 71.51\\\\%.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Deduplication statistics.}\\nBecause multiple passing solutions to the same problem produce identical (input, output) pairs in the \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} format---correct executions raise no exceptions, so the trace content is the same regardless of the solution's internal logic---the dataset contains duplicate rows.\\nTable~\\\\ref{tab:dedup} reports the duplication rates across formats.\\nTrain--test input overlap is zero (split by problem ID), so no information leakage occurs.\\nThe high duplication in \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} does not affect our experimental conclusions: after deduplication the training set still contains 2,858 unique samples and the test set 607, providing sufficient coverage for the 591-problem benchmark.\\nThe \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} test set contains 48.99\\\\% duplicate inputs due to representation compactness; all confidence intervals treat samples as independent Bernoulli trials.\\nWe report these statistics for transparency.\\n\\n\\\\begin{table}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\small\\n\\\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\\n\\\\toprule\\n\\\\textbf{Split} & \\\\textbf{Total} & \\\\textbf{Unique} & \\\\textbf{Dup.\\\\ rate} \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\midrule\\nexception\\\\_only train & 4{,}720 & 2{,}858 & 39.45\\\\% \\\\\\\\\\nexception\\\\_only test  & 1{,}190 &   607 & 48.99\\\\% \\\\\\\\\\nraw\\\\_traces (full)    & 5{,}910 & 5{,}803 &  1.81\\\\% \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\bottomrule\\n\\\\end{tabular}\\n\\\\caption{Deduplication statistics. The \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} format collapses multiple passing solutions into identical rows because correct executions produce no exception events. Train--test input overlap is 0 (no leakage).}\\n\\\\label{tab:dedup}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Trace Representations}\\n\\nWe define seven input conditions that systematically vary the trace information available to the model (Figure~\\\\ref{fig:trace_overview}):\\n\\n\\\\begin{itemize}\\n\\\\item \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace}: Source code and test input only; no execution information.\\n\\\\item \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace}: Complete line-by-line execution trace recording variable states at each step.\\n\\\\item \\\\texttt{VOTA} (Verdict-Oriented Trace Abstraction): Three compressed components---exception events, assertion-point comparisons, and loop compression summaries.\\n\\\\item \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}: Only exception and error events extracted from the trace (e.g., \\\\texttt{[ERROR] TypeError: ...}).\\n\\\\item \\\\texttt{ae\\\\_only}: Only assertion-point actual/expected value pairs from the final comparison.\\n\\\\item \\\\texttt{loop\\\\_only}: Only loop compression summaries (iteration counts and final variable states).\\n\\\\item \\\\texttt{VOTA\\\\_no\\\\_ae}: VOTA without assertion-point information (exception events + loop compression).\\n\\\\end{itemize}\\n\\nThese conditions span a spectrum from no trace information (\\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace}) through maximally compressed signals (\\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}) to the complete unfiltered trace (\\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace}).\\n\\n\\\\begin{figure*}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\includegraphics[width=\\\\textwidth]{fig1_trace_overview}\\n\\\\caption{Overview of the verdict prediction pipeline. Source code and a test input are executed to produce a full raw trace. Four experimental conditions vary the trace information provided to the model: \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} (code only, 75.13\\\\%), \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} (complete trace, 81.76\\\\%), VOTA (compressed components, 99.41\\\\%), and \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (exception events only, 99.83\\\\%). Each condition is paired with the same model to predict a pass/fail verdict. Accuracies shown are for Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B (seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$).}\\n\\\\label{fig:trace_overview}\\n\\\\end{figure*}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Models}\\n\\nWe evaluate two instruction-tuned code LLMs at the 7B parameter scale:\\n\\n\\\\begin{itemize}\\n\\\\item \\\\textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct} \\\\citep{qwen2025coder}: Pretrained on 5.5T tokens. Serves as the primary model for all ablation experiments.\\n\\\\item \\\\textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \\\\citep{deepseek2024coder}: Pretrained on 2T tokens. Used for cross-model validation to test whether findings generalize beyond a single model family.\\n\\\\end{itemize}\\n\\nThe two models differ primarily in pretraining data volume (5.5T vs.\\\\ 2T tokens), providing a natural axis for evaluating whether trace signal utility is model-dependent \\\\citep{dong2024composition}.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Training Setup}\\n\\nAll conditions use identical training configurations to ensure fair comparison.\\nWe apply LoRA \\\\citep{hu2022lora} (rank=16, $\\\\alpha$=32, dropout=0.05) to all linear layers and train for 3 epochs with batch size 4, gradient accumulation 8 (effective batch size 32), learning rate $2 \\\\times 10^{-4}$ with linear warmup and linear decay, warmup ratio 0.1, and weight decay 0.01.\\nThe maximum sequence length is 2,048 tokens; input sequences exceeding this limit are truncated from the input content while preserving the complete verdict label.\\nTraining wall-clock time ranges from 0.5 to 2.5 hours per condition on a single GPU, depending on trace format verbosity.\\nThe primary seed is 42; seed robustness is evaluated with seeds 123 and 456 for the \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} condition.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Loss Ablation}\\n\\nTo disentangle training signal quality from input signal quality, we introduce a \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace\\\\_label\\\\_only} variant.\\nThis condition uses the same full trace as input but computes loss only on the verdict token, masking all trace tokens during training (loss weight = 0 on trace positions).\\nIf this variant improves over standard \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace}, the improvement is attributable to \\\\emph{gradient dilution}---trace tokens diluting the training signal without contributing to verdict prediction.\\nThe remaining gap to VOTA is then attributable to \\\\emph{information overload}---irrelevant trace content that the model must process at inference time.\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.51}}"}
```

all confidence intervals treat samples as independent Bernoulli trials.\\nWe report these statistics for transparency.\\n\\n\\\\begin{table}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\small\\n\\\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\\n\\\\toprule\\n\\\\textbf{Split} & \\\\textbf{Total} & \\\\textbf{Unique} & \\\\textbf{Dup.\\\\ rate} \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\midrule\\nexception\\\\_only train & 4{,}720 & 2{,}858 & 39.45\\\\% \\\\\\\\\\nexception\\\\_only test  & 1{,}190 &   607 & 48.99\\\\% \\\\\\\\\\nraw\\\\_traces (full)    & 5{,}910 & 5{,}803 &  1.81\\\\% \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\bottomrule\\n\\\\end{tabular}\\n\\\\caption{Deduplication statistics. The \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} format collapses multiple passing solutions into identical rows because correct executions produce no exception events. Train--test input overlap is 0 (no leakage).}\\n\\\\label{tab:dedup}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Trace Representations}\\n\\nWe define seven input conditions that systematically vary the trace information available to the model (Figure~\\\\ref{fig:trace_overview}):\\n\\n\\\\begin{itemize}\\n\\\\item \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace}: Source code and test input only; no execution information.\\n\\\\item \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace}: Complete line-by-line execution trace recording variable states at each step.\\n\\\\item \\\\texttt{VOTA} (Verdict-Oriented Trace Abstraction): Three compressed components---exception events, assertion-point comparisons, and loop compression summaries.\\n\\\\item \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}: Only exception and error events extracted from the trace (e.g., \\\\texttt{[ERROR] TypeError: ...}).\\n\\\\item \\\\texttt{ae\\\\_only}: Only assertion-point actual/expected value pairs from the final comparison.\\n\\\\item \\\\texttt{loop\\\\_only}: Only loop compression summaries (iteration counts and final variable states).\\n\\\\item \\\\texttt{VOTA\\\\_no\\\\_ae}: VOTA without assertion-point information (exception events + loop compression).\\n\\\\end{itemize}\\n\\nThese conditions span a spectrum from no trace information (\\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace}) through maximally compressed signals (\\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}) to the complete unfiltered trace (\\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace}).\\n\\n\\\\begin{figure*}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\includegraphics[width=\\\\textwidth]{fig1_trace_overview}\\n\\\\caption{Overview of the verdict prediction pipeline. Source code and a test input are executed to produce a full raw trace. Four experimental conditions vary the trace information provided to the model: \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} (code only, 75.13\\\\%), \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} (complete trace, 81.76\\\\%), VOTA (compressed components, 99.41\\\\%), and \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (exception events only, 99.83\\\\%). Each condition is paired with the same model to predict a pass/fail verdict. Accuracies shown are for Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B (seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$).}\\n\\\\label{fig:trace_overview}\\n\\\\end{figure*}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Models}\\n\\nWe evaluate two instruction-tuned code LLMs at the 7B parameter scale:\\n\\n\\\\begin{itemize}\\n\\\\item \\\\textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct} \\\\citep{qwen2025coder}: Pretrained on 5.5T tokens. Serves as the primary model for all ablation experiments.\\n\\\\item \\\\textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \\\\citep{deepseek2024coder}: Pretrained on 2T tokens. Used for cross-model validation to test whether findings generalize beyond a single model family.\\n\\\\end{itemize}\\n\\nThe two models differ primarily in pretraining data volume (5.5T vs.\\\\ 2T tokens), providing a natural axis for evaluating whether trace signal utility is model-dependent \\\\citep{dong2024composition}.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Training Setup}\\n\\nAll conditions use identical training configurations to ensure fair comparison.\\nWe apply LoRA \\\\citep{hu2022lora} (rank=16, $\\\\alpha$=32, dropout=0.05) to all linear layers and train for 3 epochs with batch size 4, gradient accumulation 8 (effective batch size 32), learning rate $2 \\\\times 10^{-4}$ with linear warmup and linear decay, warmup ratio 0.1, and weight decay 0.01.\\nThe maximum sequence length is 2,048 tokens; input sequences exceeding this limit are truncated from the input content while preserving the complete verdict label.\\nTraining wall-clock time ranges from 0.5 to 2.5 hours per condition on a single GPU, depending on trace format verbosity.\\nThe primary seed is 42; seed robustness is evaluated with seeds 123 and 456 for the \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} condition.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Loss Ablation}\\n\\nTo disentangle training signal quality from input signal quality, we introduce a \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace\\\\_label\\\\_only} variant.\\nThis condition uses the same full trace as input but computes loss only on the verdict token, masking all trace tokens during training (loss weight = 0 on trace positions).\\nIf this variant improves over standard \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace}, the improvement is attributable to \\\\emph{gradient dilution}---trace tokens diluting the training signal without contributing to verdict prediction.\\nThe remaining gap to VOTA is then attributable to \\\\emph{information overload}---irrelevant trace content that the model must process at inference time.\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.51}}"

```text
{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Discussion}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Why Exception Events Dominate}\\n\\nVerdict prediction is fundamentally a fault detection task: the model must determine whether execution deviated from expected behavior.\\nException events are the most explicit signal of such deviation---structured diagnostic moments that encode the nature and location of a fault.\\nUnlike loop compressions or assertion-point values, which require the model to integrate information across multiple trace elements, exception events provide a self-contained verdict cue.\\nThis explains why \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} matches or exceeds VOTA: adding non-exception trace components dilutes the model's attention on the diagnostic signal rather than providing complementary information.\\n\\nWe acknowledge that exception-based verdict prediction is, at its core, fault indicator presence detection: the model learns to map the presence or absence of a structured error message to a binary label, which is close to trivially solvable for any instruction-tuned LLM.\\nThe Tier~1 accuracy of ${\\\\geq}$99.8\\\\% is therefore unsurprising in isolation.\\nThe contribution of this finding lies not in demonstrating that a strong signal yields high accuracy, but in two less obvious results: (a)~adding more trace information actively \\\\emph{hurts} performance (VOTA $<$ \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}, Table~\\\\ref{tab:ablation}), quantifying an information density dilution effect that has not been previously measured; and (b)~this dilution is confounded by tokenizer-driven trace truncation on DeepSeek: the DeepSeek tokenizer produces longer sequences than Qwen for identical inputs, causing the majority of VOTA training samples to exceed the context window (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:cross_model}); the verdict label is preserved, but the trace content is truncated, depriving the model of diagnostic information and contributing to the collapse of DeepSeek VOTA fail recall to 45.43\\\\% (Table~\\\\ref{tab:per_class}) while Qwen maintains 100\\\\%.\\n\\nThe component ablation (Table~\\\\ref{tab:ablation}) reveals a two-tier diagnostic hierarchy: Tier~1 signals (exception events, assertion points) each achieve ${\\\\geq}$99.8\\\\% individually, while Tier~2 signals (loop compression) reach only 85.6\\\\%.\\nCombining tiers in VOTA does not improve over the best Tier~1 signal, suggesting a practical design principle: invest extraction effort in preserving high-specificity diagnostic events and discard lower-tier runtime details.\\nFurther analyses of training dynamics, error patterns, and context length sensitivity are reported in Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:training_dynamics_appendix}--\\\\ref{sec:context_length_appendix}.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Limitations.}\\nOur study has several limitations.\\nFirst, all experiments use a single benchmark suite (591 problems from HumanEval and MBPP-sanitized); although DebugBench cross-dataset validation confirms the component hierarchy (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:debugbench}), generalization to other programming languages---whose exception semantics differ from Python's (e.g., Go's panic/recover, Rust's Result types)---remains untested.\\nSecond, we evaluate only two models at the ${\\\\sim}$7B parameter scale; the relationship between model size and trace signal utility is unexplored.\\nThird, DeepSeek \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} exhibits seed-dependent variance: 2/3 seeds achieve 99.33\\\\% while seed 123 yields 91.09\\\\% ($\\\\sigma{=}4.76$pp, $n{=}3$), attributable to LoRA initialization sensitivity (fail recall = 70.5\\\\% for the outlier vs.\\\\ 100\\\\% for others); Qwen shows zero variance across seeds.\\nFourth, all experiments use LoRA fine-tuning; whether full fine-tuning would eliminate seed sensitivity or alter the relative ranking of trace conditions is unknown.\\nFifth, training and test data originate from the same benchmark suite (different problems), potentially inflating accuracy estimates relative to out-of-distribution deployment.\\nFinally, the candidate solutions were generated by Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct, which also serves as one evaluation model; while this does not affect verdict prediction (classifying a \\\\emph{given} solution), the resulting class balance depends on the generator's coding ability.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Future Work.}\\nSeveral directions merit investigation: (1)~extending the component hierarchy analysis to additional languages (Java, C++, Rust) where exception and error-handling semantics differ substantially; (2)~scaling to larger models (13B, 70B) to test whether the Tier~1/Tier~2 boundary shifts with model capacity; (3)~evaluating on more diverse benchmarks including LiveCodeBench and real-world CI/CD failure logs; and (4)~deploying exception-based verdict prediction in production test pipelines to measure practical throughput and latency gains over full-trace approaches.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.53}}"}
```

```text
{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Related Work}\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Execution Traces for Code LLMs.}\\nSeveral recent efforts have incorporated execution traces into code language model training.\\nTracePile \\\\citep{chen2025tracepile} scaled trace-augmented training to 2.6M samples across multiple programming languages, demonstrating gains on code generation tasks.\\n\\\\citet{wang2025dcsh} conducted a comprehensive study of execution trace-based information for code LLMs across synthesis, repair, and reasoning tasks, finding that trace-based semantic information has limited and inconsistent utility---a finding our work extends by showing that for \\\\emph{verdict prediction}, exception events are a clear dominant signal.\\nNExT \\\\citep{ni2024next} used self-training on execution trace chain-of-thought rationales, improving program repair on MBPP by 26.1\\\\%.\\nET-CoT \\\\citep{eguchi2025etcot} introduced sub-line granularity traces via PyTracify, achieving 70.0\\\\% on CRUXEval-O with SFT on Qwen2.5-7B.\\n\\\\citet{jung2025execution} used debugger-extracted traces as grounded supervision for LLM reasoning, reducing overthinking while improving cross-domain transfer.\\n\\\\citet{ferrando2025execute} compared regular, compact, and dynamic scratchpad strategies, finding that compact traces (recording only changed variables) perform comparably to full traces---directly supporting our finding that complete value information is redundant.\\nCRUXEval \\\\citep{gu2024cruxeval} established benchmarks for code execution reasoning; CRUXEval-X \\\\citep{xu2025cruxevalx} extended this to 19 programming languages.\\nA common limitation across these studies is that they focus on generation or output prediction tasks; none have decomposed which trace components contribute to \\\\emph{classification} accuracy or tested cross-model generalization of trace signals.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Code Verdict and Patch Classification.}\\nTRACED \\\\citep{ding2024traced} showed that execution-aware pretraining improves vulnerability detection and clone detection, using encoder-based models with trace-augmented objectives.\\nIn automated patch correctness prediction, APPT \\\\citep{appt2024} fine-tuned pre-trained models with BERT+LSTM achieving 79.7\\\\% accuracy on Defects4J patches, while LLM4PatchCorrect \\\\citep{zhou2024llm4patch} used Starcoder-7B zero-shot with execution traces reaching 84.4\\\\%.\\nMore recently, \\\\citet{yang2025apsg} proposed Graph-LoRA with attributed patch semantic graphs for parameter-efficient patch classification.\\nFuseFL \\\\citep{widyasari2024fusefl} fused SBFL rankings with test execution outcomes (including error messages) for fault localization, demonstrating that structured fault signals improve over raw inputs.\\n\\\\citet{foster2025mutation} deployed LLM-based equivalence detection at scale at Meta, achieving 0.95 precision on mutation testing.\\nThese approaches treat the trace (or patch) as a monolithic input; no prior work has isolated individual trace components to determine their relative contribution to classification, or evaluated whether trace-based classifiers generalize across model families.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Trace Compression and Selection.}\\nTraceCoder \\\\citep{huang2026tracecoder} inserts diagnostic probes at selected locations rather than recording full traces, improving Pass@1 by 34.4\\\\% for LLM-generated code debugging.\\nInspectCoder \\\\citep{wang2025inspectcoder} enables LLMs to actively control a debugger for strategic breakpoint placement and runtime state inspection, outperforming baselines by 5--60\\\\%.\\nND-Slicer \\\\citep{yadavally2024ndslicer} uses execution-aware pretraining to predict dynamic program slices without execution, achieving 81.3\\\\% exact match.\\n\\\\citet{haque2025traces} found that raw execution traces provide limited benefit for program repair and that LLM-optimized prompt formats consistently outperform raw traces---supporting our finding that trace compression is essential.\\n\\\\citet{wu2026runtime} used selective variable snapshots in a multi-agent repair debate framework, further validating that targeted trace extraction outperforms full recording.\\nThese methods target repair tasks (generating fixes) rather than classification, and none analyze the information density trade-off between trace completeness and prediction accuracy.\\nOur information density decomposition---separating gradient dilution (26\\\\%) from information overload (74\\\\%)---provides the first quantitative framework for understanding \\\\emph{why} trace compression helps, complementing these methods' focus on \\\\emph{how} to compress.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.43}}"}
```

```text
{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Conclusion}\\n\\nWe presented the first systematic study of what execution trace components drive verdict prediction performance.\\nThrough component ablation across two code LLMs, we established three findings:\\n\\n\\\\textbf{(1) Exception signal transfer.}\\nException events alone achieve 99.83\\\\% $\\\\pm$ 0.00 on Qwen (3 seeds) and 99.33\\\\% on 2/3 DeepSeek seeds, with one outlier at 91.09\\\\% from LoRA initialization sensitivity.\\nEven the outlier exceeds VOTA (84.45\\\\%) by 6.64pp, confirming exception events as the most effective component across models; training stability is a secondary factor for smaller models.\\nIn contrast, the full VOTA representation degrades by 14.96pp on DeepSeek---a gap confounded by training-time trace truncation (the majority of DeepSeek VOTA samples exceed the context window; \\\\S\\\\ref{sec:cross_model}) and model capability differences, which cannot be disentangled in the current design.\\n\\n\\\\textbf{(2) Information density decomposition.}\\nThe 17.65pp gap between full traces and VOTA decomposes into gradient dilution (26\\\\%, from training on irrelevant trace tokens) and information overload (74\\\\%, from processing irrelevant trace lines at inference).\\nThe primary bottleneck is trace content selection, not loss design.\\n\\n\\\\textbf{(3) Two-tier diagnostic hierarchy.}\\nTier~1 signals (exception events, assertion points) individually achieve ${\\\\geq}$99.8\\\\% accuracy, while Tier~2 signals (loop compression) reach 85.6\\\\%.\\nCombining tiers in VOTA slightly degrades performance (99.41\\\\%), confirming that additional trace components introduce noise rather than complementary signal.\\n\\nThese results yield a practical design principle: for verdict prediction, extract the highest-specificity diagnostic signal and discard everything else.\\nFuture work should test whether exception dominance extends to other datasets (DebugBench, LiveCodeBench), model scales, and classification tasks beyond verdict prediction.\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.44}}"}
```
